## 1. Background information on the procedure

## 1.1. Type II variation

Pursuant to Article 7.2 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 1234/2008, Novartis Europharm Limited submitted to the European Medicines Agency on 22 August 2025 an application for group of variations.

The following variations were requested in the group:

| Variation(s) requested   | Variation(s) requested                                                      | Type              |
|--------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------|
| C.I.6.a                  | Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Variation type II |
| C.I.6.a                  | Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Variation type II |

Grouping of two Type II C.I.6 variations to support the extension of the LEQVIO indication to paediatric patients aged 12 to less than 18 years with heterozygous and homozygous familial hypercholesterolaemia, as follows:

C.I.6: Extension of indication to include the treatment of paediatric patients aged 12 to less than 18 years with heterozygous familial hypercholesterolaemia (HeFH) for LEQVIO based on the final results from study CKJX839C12301 (ORION-16). ORION-16 is a two part (double-blind inclisiran versus placebo [Year 1] followed by open-label inclisiran [Year 2]) randomised multicentre study to evaluate safety, tolerability, and efficacy of inclisiran in paediatric patients (12 to less than 18 years) with heterozygous familial hypercholesterolemia and elevated LDL-cholesterol.

C.I.6: Extension of indication to include the treatment of paediatric patients aged 12 to less than 18 years with homozygous familial hypercholesterolaemia (HoFH) for LEQVIO based on the final results from study CKJX839C12302 (ORION-13). ORION-13 is a two part (double-blind inclisiran versus placebo [Year 1] followed by open-label inclisiran [Year 2]) randomised multicentre study to evaluate safety, tolerability, and efficacy of inclisiran in paediatric patients (12 to less than 18 years) with homozygous familial hypercholesterolemia and elevated LDL-cholesterol.

As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.8, 5.1, and 5.2 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 4.0 of the RMP has also been submitted.

The requested variation(s) proposed amendments to the Summary of Product Characteristics (SmPC) and Package Leaflet (PL) and to the Risk Management Plan (RMP).

## Information on paediatric requirements

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included (an) EMA Decision(s) P/0322/2023 on the agreement of a paediatric investigation plan (PIP). The original PIP P/0321/2018 was modified twice, on 2 December 2022 (EMA decision P/0495/2022) and on 11 August 2023 (EMA decision P/0322/2023).

At the time of submission of the application, the PIP P/0322/2023 was not yet completed as some measures were deferred.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

EMA issued on 25 April 2025 a positive partial compliance check for the PIP P/0322/2023 confirming the completion of Study 1, ORION- 13 (CKJX839C12302) and Study 2, identifier ORION-16 (CKJX839C1230).

## Information relating to orphan market exclusivity

## Similarity

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No. 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the MAH did not submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products because there is no authorised orphan medicinal product for a condition related to the proposed indication.

## Derogation(s) of market exclusivity

N/A

## Scientific advice

The MAH did not seek Scientific Advice at the CHMP.

## 2. Scientific discussion

## 2.1. Introduction

## 2.1.1. Problem statement

## Disease or condition

Familial hypercholesterolemia (FH) is a genetic disorder of lipoprotein metabolism manifested by lifelong and marked elevation in LDL cholesterol (LDL-C) levels from birth. If left untreated, it is characterised by premature cardiovascular disease (CVD). FH is caused by variants in genes encoding proteins which regulate LDLR-mediated clearance of LDL-C; there are two clinical manifestations, heterozygous FH (HeFH) and the more severe homozygous FH (HoFH), depending on the presence of one or two affected alleles in these genes.

## State the claimed the therapeutic indication (new: underlined)

Primary hypercholesterolaemia and mixed dyslipidaemia

Leqvio is indicated in adults with primary hypercholesterolaemia (heterozygous familial and nonfamilial) or mixed dyslipidaemia, as an adjunct to diet, and in paediatric patients aged 12 years and older with heterozygous familial hypercholesterolaemia (HeFH):

- in combination with a statin or statin with other lipid-lowering therapies in patients unable to reach LDL-C goals with the maximum tolerated dose of a statin, or
- alone or in combination with other lipid-lowering therapies in patients who are statin-intolerant, or for whom a statin is contraindicated.

Homozygous familial hypercholesterolaemia (HoFH)

Leqvio is indicated in paediatric patients aged 12 to less than 18 years with homozygous familial hypercholesterolaemia in combination with other lipid-lowering therapies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Epidemiology

The overall disease prevalence for HeFH is currently estimated as about 1 in every 250 - 300 people (Beheshti et al 2020 1 , Hu et al 2020 2 , Cuchel et al 2023 3 ), while HoFH is a rare disease with an estimated prevalence of 1 in every 250,000 - 360,000 people (Cuchel et al 2023).

## Aetiology and pathogenesis

The three main known genetic mutations in familial hypercholesterolemia are classified as defects in the LDL receptor ([LDLR]; most common), apolipoprotein B (ApoB), or proprotein convertase subtilisin / kexin type 9 (PCSK9). Each of these three mutations leads to impairment of LDL receptors and a reduction in uptake of LDL-cholesterol (LDL-C) and subsequently causes high LDL-C concentration. Patients can have a heterozygous or a homozygous defect, which will determine the severity of the disease and the age of onset of cardiovascular (CV) disease manifestations. Children who carry both defect genes from two heterozygote parents almost do not have LDLR to uptake LDL-C and subsequently present with extremely high LDL-C levels and early-onset CV disease. Over 1600 gene mutations in LDLR are detected in 85% to 90% of patients with familiar hypercholesterolaemia (FH). ApoB mutations account for about 10% of patients with FH, and from this amount, the Arg3500Gln gene mutation is the most common cause. PCSK9 gene mutation causes less than 5% of patients with FH. Moreover, only severe mutations in PCSK9 can lead to FH. However, a less common mutation in signal-transducing adaptor family member 1 (STAP1) was also reported to cause FH. (Vaezi et al 2025 4 )

## Clinical presentation

LDL-C levels in affected individuals are elevated and in spite of aggressive statin use there is still a 2fold excess of coronary heart disease (CHD) related deaths relative to age-matched controls within this population. A strong positive correlation and causal relationship between LDL-C levels and risk of CV disease (CVD) has been derived from a large body of clinical and epidemiological studies. There is also a strong relationship to other clinical manifestations in the body like cerebrovascular disease (i.e. ischaemic stroke) or peripheral vascular disease. Epidemiologic data indicate a continuous increasing relative risk of CVD from very low to high levels of LDL-C, with a higher absolute risk in patients at the higher end of LDL-C levels.

## Management

The underlying atherosclerotic vascular lesions that cause CHD, CVD, and peripheral vascular occlusive disease are caused by an elevated LDL-C level. An outstanding role to reduce the risk of major CV events is lowering of LDL-C, which has been established for a long time. Data to support the relationship between LDL-C and CV events and specifically the relationship with inhibition of PCSK9 has been shown in genetic studies of variations in the genes that influence LDL-C and from studies in patients with FH. In Mendelian randomisation studies evaluating multiple genetic targets that impact LDL-C levels, the variants in genes that regulate LDL-C levels (e.g., LDL-R, PCSK9) have demonstrated that lowering of LDL-C is associated with a lower risk for CV events. To minimise this risk factor, lipidlowering therapies are recommended. To reach this goal, specific targets have been suggested. It has been shown that the higher the CV risks, the more aggressive the lipid-lowering therapy should be.

1 Beheshti S, Madsen C, Varbo A, et al (2020) Worldwide prevalence of familial hypercholesterolemia: Meta-analyses of 11 million subjects. JACC; (20):2553-66.

2 Hu P, Dharmayat KI, Stevens CAT, et al (2020) Prevalence of familial hypercholesterolemia among the general population and patients with atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Circulation; 141(22):1742-59.

3 Cuchel M, Raal FJ, Hegele RA, et al (2023) 2023 Update on European Atherosclerosis Society Consensus Statement on Homozygous Familial Hypercholesterolaemia: New Treatments and Clinical Guidance. Eur Heart J; 44(25):2277-91.

4 Vaezi Z, Amini A. Familial Hypercholesterolemia. [Updated 2022 Sep 26]. In: StatPearls [Internet]. Treasure Island (FL): StatPearls Publishing; 2025 Jan-. Available from: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK556009/

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Early initiation of treatment in patients with FH is considered crucial for ASCVD risk reduction (Reijman et al 2021 5 ). Despite the availability of multiple options of lipid-lowering therapies, including statins, ezetimibe, and newer treatments like anti-PCSK9 or anti-ANGPTL3 (HoFH only) monoclonal antibodies, many patients with HeFH or HoFH, including adolescents, do not reach recommended LDL-C goals and a relevant unmet medical need is considered to remain, in particular for the young patient population, who is at high risk of future CV events and thus requires intensive management of hypercholesterolemia. In addition, as adherence to lipid-lowering therapies is known to be a particular challenge in adolescents (Joyce et al 2016 6 , Langslet et al 2021 7 , Weinstock et al 2023 8 ), effective treatments to reduce LDL-C, which do not require frequent self-administration, might be especially beneficial in this age group.

## 2.1.2. About the product

Inclisiran is a cholesterol-lowering, double-stranded, small interfering ribonucleic acid (siRNA), conjugated on the sense strand with triantennary N-acetylgalactosamine (GalNAc) to facilitate uptake by hepatocytes. In hepatocytes, inclisiran utilises the RNA interference mechanism and directs catalytic breakdown of mRNA for proprotein convertase subtilisin kexin type 9. This increases LDL-C receptor recycling and expression on the hepatocyte cell surface, which increases LDL-C uptake and lowers LDLC levels in the circulation.

The currently approved therapeutic indication is primary hypercholesterolaemia (heterozygous familial and non-familial) or mixed dyslipidaemia in adults as an adjunct to diet:

- in combination with a statin or statin with other lipid-lowering therapies in patients unable to reach LDL-C goals with the maximum tolerated dose of a statin, or
- alone or in combination with other lipid-lowering therapies in patients who are statinintolerant, or for whom a statin is contraindicated.

The recommended dose is 284 mg inclisiran administered as a single subcutaneous injection initially, again at 3 months, and then followed by every 6 months.

## 2.1.3. The development programme / compliance with CHMP guidance / scientific advice

The Paediatric Investigation Plan (PIP) for inclisiran was first approved on 12 September 2018 (EMA decision P/0321/2018); the original PIP was modified twice, on 2 December 2022 (EMA decision P/0495/2022) and on 11 August 2023 (EMA decision P/0322/2023).

At the time of submission of the application, the PIP has not yet been completed as some measures have been deferred.

5 Reijman MD, Kusters DM, Wiegman A (2021) Advances in familial hypercholesterolaemia in children. Lancet Child Adolesc Health. 5(9):652-61.

6 Joyce NR, Wellenius GA, Eaton CB, et al. (2016). Patterns and predictors of medication adherence to lipid-lowering therapy in children aged 8 to 20 years. J Clin Lipidol; 10(4):824-32.

7 Langslet G, Johansen AK, Bogsrud MP, et al. (2021). Thirty percent of children and young adults with familial hypercholesterolemia treated with statins have adherence issues. Am J Prev Cardiol; 6:100180.

8 Weinstock RS, Trief PM, Burke BK, et al. (2023). Antihypertensive and lipid-lowering medication adherence in young adults with youth-onset type 2 diabetes. JAMA Netw Open; 6(10):e2336964.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A partial compliance check was completed on 25 April 2025, confirming compliance with the latest PIP P/0322/2023 (Compliance Report EMA/PE/0000248841). The partial compliance check was performed on the two PIP studies that were completed before the submission of the new paediatric indications in adolescents with HeFH and HoFH, study ORION-13 (CKJX839C12302) (study 1) in paediatric patients from ≥12 years to &lt;18 years of age with homozygous familial hypercholesterolaemia (HoFH) and study ORION-16 (CKJX839C12301) (study 2) in paediatric patients from ≥12 years to &lt;18 years of age with heterozygous familial hypercholesterolaemia (HeFH).

The currently approved PIP also contains two additional clinical studies in younger children from ≥6 years to &lt;12 years of age with HeFH and HoFH.

## 2.1.4. General comments on compliance with GCP

In both study reports, ORION-13 (CKJX839C12302) and ORION-16 (CKJX839C12301), it is stated that these studies were conducted in compliance with Good Clinical Practice (GCP).

The Applicant provides a fully revised ERA according to EMA guideline 2024 including the available study reports validated in 2021 (EMEA/H/C/5333/IB/07).

All study reports were valid and plausible. The PEC calculation was adapted for the requirements in the revised guideline. For surface water, groundwater and sewage treatment no risks are identified. Inclisiran is vP but not PBT or vPvB. Assessment for soil and a calculation for secondary poisoning is not needed. The risk for the sediment compartment was identified by the Assessor in Tier A by strictly following the guideline approach. However, the Applicant provided sufficient evidence to underpin the assumption that the proposed test designs are not suitable to this large molecule. Therefore, based on consideration of all fate and sediment effect tests provided, it is concluded that a risk to sediment organisms seems unlikely until new approaches are available for these kinds of molecules.

## 2.2.2. Discussion and conclusion on the non-clinical aspects

## Environmental Risk Assessment

Considering the above data from Phase I and Phase II, inclisiran is not expected to pose a risk to the environment. Considering the above data of the definitive hazard assessment, inclisiran is not a PBT or vPvB substance.

## Pharmacokinetics

## Determination of inclisiran (KJX839) in human K2EDTA plasma

Inclisiran (also known as KJX839) was quantified from K2EDTA human plasma using the test items A120190 (antisense strand of KJX839) and A-122088 (sense strand of KJX839) following solid phase extraction (SPE) by means of a liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) method with internal standard ALN-60519 (mixture of A-122230 sense strand (IS for A-120190) and A-122227 antisense strand (IS for A-122088)) across a validated concentration range of 10.0 ng/mL and 10000 ng/mL using 100 µL of plasma (analytical method code INCLHPP).

LabCorp Early Development Laboratories Inc. validated a bioanalytical method (INCLHPP (-MAD)) for the quantitative detection of A-120190 (antisense strand) and A- 122088 (sense strand) of the KJX839 duplex using internal standard ALN-60519. No statement on compliance with regulatory guidelines was made; however, acceptance criteria were specified and appear to be overall compliant with the Guideline on bioanalytical method validation [EMEA/CHMP/EWP/192217/2009 Rev. 1 Corr. 2**] applicable at the time of conduct.

Using single stranded analyte reference standards (A-120190 and A-122088), calibration standards (n=8) were prepared at 10 ng/mL, 20 ng/mL, 80 ng/mL, 300 ng/mL, 1000 ng/mL, 4500 ng/mL, 9000 ng/mL and 10000 ng/mL) and QC samples at LLOQ QC: 10.0 ng/mL, Low QC: 30.0 ng/mL, Lowmedium QC: 400 ng/mL, Mid-QC: 5,000 ng/mL, HQC: 8,000 ng/mL, and DQC: 100,000 ng/mL (diluted by factor of 100x with blank plasma).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Using single stranded analyte reference standards (A-120190 and A-122088), calibration standards (n=8) were prepared at 10 ng/mL, 20 ng/mL, 80 ng/mL, 300 ng/mL, 1000 ng/mL, 4500 ng/mL, 9000 ng/mL and 10000 ng/mL) and QC samples at LLOQ QC: 10.0 ng/mL, Low QC: 30.0 ng/mL, Lowmedium QC: 400 ng/mL, Mid-QC: 5,000 ng/mL, HQC: 8,000 ng/mL, and DQC: 100,000 ng/mL (diluted by factor of 100x with blank plasma).

Validation met specified acceptance criteria for selectivity, intra-day and inter-day accuracy and precision, effect of haemolysis and hyperlipidaemia, matrix effect and recovery. Somewhat conflicting information was given regarding carryover. According to the \"summary of method validation\" included in the report, validation of carryover fulfilled acceptance criteria; however, validation of carryover is not listed in the validation run summary table 5-1 of the report, and according to the Method Specific Requirements in report amendment 1 \"significant column carryover was occasionally observed with extracted batches. To lower the carryover, the post elution gradient was modified during the validation. Still, the recommendation was issued to use special sample ordering and extra carryover blanks as a precaution, and to ensure that extracted samples are completely dry prior to reconstitution to help minimise carryover\".

Extended long-term stability in matrix was reported in report amendment 1 [DMPK-R2000084-pk-01] to be 623 Days for A-120190 (antisense strand) and 636 Days for A-122088 (sense strand). Of note, the A-122088 run acceptance QCs in runs 15-17 trended or failed due to low %bias for the LMQC and LQC levels and the LQC long term stability QCs consistently failed due to low %bias in these same runs. An evaluation was started and a preparation issue for the A-122088 analyte was suspected as possible cause of the stability failures. New stock and intermediate solutions were prepared and the long-term stability assessment was repeated in run 18 where it met acceptance for A-122088.

Overall, the method was concluded to be suitable for the determination of A-120190 and A-122088 concentrations in human K2EDTA plasma over the range of inclisiran concentrations of 10.0 (LLOQ) to 10,000 (ULOQ) ng/mL using 100 µL of human plasma.

Method description INCLHPP, version 6, effective date 22 Sep 2022 was included in Appendix C of amendment No.1 DMPK R2000084-pk-01). Review of chromatography is mentioned for several runs in the report. It was stated regarding integration parameters that \"a small amount of crosstalk is observed between compounds even with strict m/z mass tolerance criteria (&lt; 10 ppm). The interferences peaks are baseline separated since the internal standards are analogues, but careful attention to integration parameters is still required to exclude the nearby peak; adjusting the baseline window to &lt; 20 was typically sufficient during method development\". In response to second response for a supplementary information (RSI), relevant Labcorp Policies and SOPs were provided and the applicant confirmed that no manual (re-)integration occurred. However, based on the Applicant's response, it was not possible to fully verify correct peak integration claimed by the Applicant. Possible implications: selectivity of the method, matrix effect, carryover; potentially impacting the validity of pharmacokinetic data.

Furthermore, the bioanalytical method (INCLHPP) and the contract facility (Labcorp) used in the ORION-16 and ORION-13 studies differ from the method (LC-TOF-MS) and contract facility (QPS LLC, Delaware Technology Park, 3 Innovation Way, Suite 240, Newark, DE 19711 (actual testing facility QPS LCC, 110 Executive Drive, Suite 7 Newark, DE 19702, USA)) used in the earlier PK studies supporting the original approval. According to ICH M10, cross validation is required to demonstrate how the reported data are related when multiple bioanalytical methods and/or multiple bioanalytical laboratories are involved. Since the Applicant compares the inclisiran plasma concentration data of the ORION-16/ORION-13 studies with the earlier data (i.e. with data reported in PK Report ALN-PCSSC001-Table 15.1.1.1.5) and additionally combines them in a population PK analysis for inclisiran (report KJX839C Population PK report), cross validation data is necessary in support. In response to second RSI, the applicant clarified that there is no direct cross-validation comparing LabCorp Madison single strand method INCLHPP, validated in [DMPK R2000084-pk], [DMPK R2000084-pk-01] and applied in the ORION-16 and ORIOIN-13 studies, and original QPS duplex assay method 319-1418. Instead, method comparison is to rely on a chain of separate cross-validations, first DMPK R2200200-pk, crossvalidating original QPS duplex assay method 319-1418 with LabCorp Madison duplex assay 839DHPP, and second DMPK R2200365-pk, cross-validating LabCorp Madison duplex assay 839DHPP with LabCorp Madison single strand method INCLHPP. In this indirect comparison, LabCorp Madison duplex assay 839DHPP that has not yet been applied to study sample analysis has to serve as \"bridge\".

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

According to weighted Deming regression, LabCorp Madison duplex assay results are generally higher than those at QPS with difference increasing with concentration. Bland-Altman Plot of mean percentage difference revealed that while overall mean is higher, Labcorp Madison duplex results are lower than those for QPS in the lower concentration range (i.e. at LQC (30ng/mL) and LMQC (400ng/mL)) and higher in the higher concentration range (i.e. at MQC (5000ng/mL) and HQC (8000ng/mL)).

Weighted Deming regression analysis of intra-lab cross-validation comparing LabCorp Madison duplex assay method 839DHPP with LabCorp Madison single strand assay INCLHPP also submitted in response to RSI (KJX839-Statistical Memo) revealed proportional and fixed bias for A-120190 (the predicted Value 2 INCLHPP Converted Duplex (Y) for a given Value 1 839DHPP (X) is: Y = (1.132 * X) - 6.818), but no evidence of proportional and fixed bias for A-122088 and the duplex resp. converted duplex, respectively. While the latter may be considered reassuring, in absence of a direct cross-validation, a stronger bias for the Labcorp Madison single strand assay INCLHPP than detected for the Labcorp Madison duplex assay 839DHPP vs. the original QPS duplex assay 319-1418 cannot be ruled out.

However, given that most samples reported in the ORON-16 and -13 studies had a measured concentration below 2000 ng/mL, the impact on PK data was considered acceptable.

It was concluded that there was no impact to the study data since the INCLHPP assay has -20°C long-term frozen matrix stability established for at least 623 days for A-120190 and 636 days for A-122088.

## KJX839 Conversion

The concentration of KJX839 duplex was determined from the measured A-120190 concentration using an equation. To obtain the duplex concentration all measured A-120190 concentrations were multiplied by the conversion factor.

The concentration of KJX839 duplex was determined from the measured A-122088 using an equation. To obtain the duplex concentration the measured A-122088 concentration was multiplied by the conversion factor.

KJX839 duplex concentrations were reported based on A-120190 (antisense strand) / A-122088 (sense strand) ratio. For any ratio which cannot be calculated, such as antisense or sense is BQL, the concentration of KJX839 is reported as BQL.

No statement on compliance with regulatory guidelines, i.e. Guideline on bioanalytical method validation [EMEA/CHMP/EWP/192217/2009 Rev. 1 Corr. 2**] and/or ICH M10 effective date 21/01/2023 was made; however, run acceptance criteria were specified and appear to be overall compliant with regulatory recommendations. The applicant clarified with the responses to the RSI, that no manual (re) integration took place in the ORION-16 study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

According to the ORION-16 CSR, the most common protocol deviations (reported in 3/141 (2.1%) participants each) were in the category \"other\", specifically PK sample(s) being taken either before a separate consent signature to the main informed consent form was provided (2 participants (2.2%) in the inclisiran group and 1 participant (2.1%) in the placebo group) or without a separate informed consent (3 participants (3.2%) in the inclisiran group); samples without consent were not used.

In response to a RSI regarding sample disposition, the applicant provided an amended bioanalytical report for the ORION-16 study to correctly reflect the number of samples analysed vs. received. Sample disposition was further clarified in response to a second RSI, explaining the different number of reported sample concentrations between the amended bioanalytical report, the ORION-16 CSR Listing 16.2.6-1.5i and the ORION-16 CSR tables Tables 11-10 and 14.3-9.1i.

As part of concomitant lipid modifying therapies as well as for treatment of AEs, concomitant medications were administered to study participants (see i.a. ORION-16 CSR Table 14.3-2.6i). In response to an RSI, the applicant clarified that assay interference by concomitant medication was neither validated nor considered necessary based on the signal (i.e., for inclisiran detection) used for inclisiran being clearly different from any low molecular weight treatment.

According to the run summary provided in Table 5-1 of the bioanalytical report, in run 2, LQCs for analyte A-120190 did not meet acceptance criteria and the run was reinjected in run 3 for quantification of A-120190. The applicant clarified in response to a RSI that the failure was due to a technical cause. According to ICH M10 in cases where the failure is due to an assignable technical cause, samples may be reinjected if reinjection reproducibility has been demonstrated (sufficient on injector stability provided). In response to second RSI, the Applicant substantiated successful validation of reinjection reproducibility.

Of note, because of carryover observed during the pre-study method validation, a recommendation was issued in the method validation report to use special sample ordering and extra carryover blanks as a precaution. In study sample analysis, substantial carryover was reported in all five analytical runs, and substantial interference was detectable in run blanks. According to the report, in each run there were also blanks with acceptable interference. The Applicant's concluded and confirmed this once more in response to an RSI that no sample data was impacted by &gt;5% due to the observed carryover.

## Immunogenicity

Detection of anti-inclisiran-reactive IgG/IgM antibodies in normal human serum (anti-drug antibodies [ADA] measurement / immunogenicity assessment)

For ADA measurements, a qualitative enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) for the detection of anti-inclisiran reactive immunoglobulin (Ig)G/IgM antibodies in clinical serum samples was developed by Charles River Laboratories Montreal ULC Senneville Site and was previously validated under study numbers 3000701 and 3001805.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The method was designed to detect the presence of ADA in human serum in a tiered approach, containing three components: (1) screening assay, which identifies samples as negative or potential positive; (2) confirmatory assay, which assesses specificity of the samples screened as potential positive; and (3) titration assay, which estimates the titer of ADA for the confirmed positive samples.

Method validation reports 3000701 and 3001805 were assessed previously during the initial marketing authorisation approval of Leqvio. It was concluded that the performance of the assay to detect ADA towards inclisiran is acceptable and has been adequately validated.

As part of sequence 0061 supporting the current variation, method validation report amendment 1 (DMPK R2100159-ig-01) (Charles River Laboratories Montreal ULC study number 3002412), dated 12 Apr 2023 was submitted.

Report DMPK R2100159-ig-01 presented results of partial validation, with the study plan (amendment 5), CoAs, analytical procedure (AP.3002412.Ab.05) and method validation summary appended to the report.

The experimental phase start date was 18-May-2022, and the experimental phase completion date was 25-Nov-2022.

A compliance statement was included stating that the validation study was performed in accordance with the OECD Principles of GLP and that there were no deviations from these that affected the overall integrity of the study, the results and conclusions.

For the validated method, human serum samples, ADA present in the human serum samples bound to the The detection of inclisiran-bound human anti-inclisiran IgG and/or IgM antibodies present in the test samples was performed using a goat anti-human IgG + IgM (H+L)-horseradish peroxidase (HRP) conjugated secondary antibody. The HRP metabolised the subsequently added tetramethylbenzidine to generate a chromophore, for which the absorbance at 450 nm (A450nm) was determined using a spectrophotometer. The A450nm of the chromophore correlated with the level of human anti-inclisiran IgG/IgM antibodies present in the human samples.

The positive controls (PC) were (i).

The screening assay sensitivity was defined at 489 ng/mL and the low positive control (LPC) set at 600 ng/mL for screening and confirmatory assay. The screening cut point factor (SCPF) was set at 1.37 and the confirmatory cut point (CCP) at 31.03% as summarised in below table.

The modified method using the showed comparable results to the previous assay using the in 85% of samples tested in the cross-validation [DMPK R2100159-ig-01]. Hence, it was concluded that the methods were successfully cross-validated.

DMPK R2100159-ig-01 method validation results mostly passed acceptance criteria with some exceptions, i.e. overall, 4.1% of the assays (3 out of 74) failed to meet the assay acceptance criteria during the validation.

Despite the observed assay limitations, the assay (original and modified) were considered overall acceptably validated and suitable for ADA measurement in clinical studies ORION-16 and ORION-13. In Part 1 (double-blind part) in ORION-16 and ORION-13 studies, ADA were detected with the original method based on [DMPK R2100159-ig-01]. Due to the unavailability of the in Part 2 (open labelled part) in ORION-16 and ORION-13 studies, ADA were detected with the method based on [DMPK R2100159-ig-01]. As discussed in the validation report assay limitations, the sensitivity values were lower with the modified assay using same day direct coating than using the original coating conditions and results of cross-validation supported an overall greater sensitivity of the modified assay. While a low false positive rate is desirable, it is false negative results that are unacceptable. Since no positive samples would be missed using the same day coating method as correctly pointed out in the validation report, the overall greater sensitivity of the modified assay may be considered acceptable.

ADA study sample analysis and in-study validation in the ORION-16 study (Bioanalytical report DMPK RCKJX839C12301-ig [Test site Study No. 3002164])

For the analysis of formation of ADA to inclisiran, whole blood study samples were collected in part 1 on Day 1 prior to study drug (inclisiran or placebo) injection (referred to as Day 1 0h) and 4 hours after injection (referred to as Day 1 +4h), as well as on Day 150 and Day 330. Unscheduled samples and Early Termination (ET) samples collected prior to Day 360 (due to unscheduled visits or early termination/premature discontinuation) were also collected and analysed.

In Part 2 (Year 2), samples for the ADA analysis collected on Day 510 and Day 720.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Whole blood study samples from the ORION-16 Clinical Study No. CKJX839C12301 were processed into serum and recovered serum study samples were shipped to the Test Site on dry ice and were stored at the Test Site in a freezer set to maintain -80°C until the end of the study.

The analytical phase experimental start date was 01-Dec-2022 and the analytical phase experimental completion date was 16-Dec-2024.

According to the report, all human serum samples tested in this study phase were analysed within validated bench-top (BT) and freeze-thaw (F/T) stabilities, unless reported as deviation. The long-term storage of study samples at -80°C is supported by the literature (Boridy, et al. 2019).

Deviations were reported relating to a subset of samples undergoing a documented temperature excursion of unknown duration prior to analysis at the Test Site according to the shipping manifest information. Since the results obtained out of stability were not reported and affected profiles were considered to be adequately interpretable without the data from affected samples, it was concluded that these deviations had no impact on the integrity of the overall study data and were considered to have no impact on the ADA results interpretation.

## Sample disposition

A total of 827 samples from 141 subjects were analysed, including 551 samples collected during Part 1 (Year 1) and 273 samples collected during Part 2 (Year 2). For 3 samples (001 Day 330, 002 Day 720 and 003 Day 720), the analysis was considered to have been performed outside of the validated BenchTop stability of the sample and reported as deviation and no result is reported (see deviation above).

From the remaining 824 samples analysed in the screening tier, 112 samples from 53 subjects had a mean A450nm value ≥ PSCP (plate-specific cut point) and were determined to be potentially positive (The A450nm values obtained for the study samples varied from 0.044 to 0.189 for those that screened negative and from 0.152 to 0.406 for those that screened potentially positive) and were reanalysed in the confirmatory assay. In the confirmatory assay, 12 out of the 112 reanalysed samples were determined to be positive. All confirmed positive samples were collected from 8 different subjects at two of the visits (Day 510 and Day 720). The total incidence of ADA was 5.7% (8 out of 141 subjects).

[Source DMPK RCKJX839C12301-ig, Table 7-1)

The false positive rate (FPR) at the screening level was assessed by considering 140 samples collected from 140 subjects prior to injection on Day 1 (Day 1 0h) as baseline samples. 16 out of these were potentially positive in the screening tier and negative in the confirmatory tier. The FPR at the screening level was 11.4 % which was at the higher end of the expected FPR range of 2 to 11% for study sample testing (Devanarayan V, 2017). Since a confirmatory assay was in place to discriminate between true and false positive, this was considered acceptable. For subject 004, no baseline sample was analysed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The 12 study samples tested positive in the confirmatory assay were collected from 8 subjects (005, 006, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011and 012) and were analysed in the titration tier. The titre values (dilution fold) ranged from &lt; 50 (MRD) to 400. The titer values obtained were ≤ 50 (7 samples, 58.3%), 100 (3 samples, 25.0%) or 400 (2 samples, 16.7%; both collected from subject 006).

Since the baseline (Day 1 0h) samples for all 8 of these subjects were all determined to be negative, it was concluded that the presence of a positive Day 510 and/or Day 720 sample in the ADA profile indicated a treatment-related immunogenicity response. Persistent and transient ADA were assessed using the definitions proposed by Shankar et al (2014).

As per clinical protocol, a dosing visit occurred at Day 360 and subjects in the placebo group would have received their first dose of inclisiran at this visit, potentially explaining the greater prevalence of positive samples collected during Part 2 (Year 2) of the clinical study. Since the Part 2 (Year 2) samples were analysed using the same-day coating method, it should be noted that the same-day coating method has a superior sensitivity to the method used for Part 1 samples (refer to Test Site Study No. 3002412). According to the Applicant, the results obtained for the baseline samples (assessed using the coating method) suggest that both coating methods were able to detect ADA appropriately in the samples.

Study sample analysis has been adequately reported. The confirmatory controls from reported assays met the acceptance criteria. The FPR at the screening level was 11.4 %, considerably higher than desirable (preferably 5%); however, the Applicant's reasoning that a confirmatory assay was in place to discriminate between true and false positive may be considered reassuring. During validation (Test Site Study No. 3002412), drug tolerance was established at ≤ 1 µg/mL of inclisiran at the sensitivity low positive control (sLPC) level. Therefore, analysis of any study sample with inclisiran concentrations &gt; 1 μg/mL could have yielded false-negative results. The Applicant pointed out that the drug tolerance is highly dependent upon the affinity of the positive control and may not be representative for all study samples.

## ADA study sample analysis and in-study validation in the ORION-13 study (Bioanalytical report DMPK RCKJX839C12302-ig [Test site Study No. 3002165])

For the analysis of formation of ADA to inclisiran, whole blood study samples samples were collected in part 1 on Day 1 prior to study drug (inclisiran or placebo) injection (referred to as Day 1 0h) and 4 hours after injection (referred to as Day 1 +4h), as well as on Day 150 and Day 330. In Part 2 (Year 2), samples for the ADA analysis collected on Day 510 and Day 720 (End of Treatment/Study, EOS).

Whole blood study samples from the ORION-13 Clinical Study No. CKJX839C12302 were processed into serum and recovered serum study samples were shipped to the Test Site on dry ice and were stored at the Test Site in a freezer set to maintain -80°C until the end of the study.

The analytical phase experimental start date was 05-Dec-2022 and the analytical phase experimental completion date was 18-Dec-2024.

According to the report, all human serum samples tested in this study phase were analysed within validated bench-top (BT) and freeze-thaw (F/T) stabilities. The long-term storage of study samples at - 80°C is supported by the literature (Boridy, et al. 2019).

Besides minor deviations that were captured in the raw data records and not considered to have impacted the overall integrity of the study phase nor the interpretation of the results and conclusions, one notable deviation from the Laboratory Phase Plan and one notable deviation from the Analytical Procedure were reported. Firstly, wrong assay plates were used to perform two assays (Prod-13 and Prod-14) contrary to the Laboratory Phase Plan, causing assay failure. As expected from this deviation, Prod-13 and Prod-14 failed to meet the run acceptance criteria due to low signals for the positive controls (sLPC, LPC and HPC) and were rejected. Since the results were not reported, this deviation had no impact on the validity of the study phase sample analysis results.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Secondly, wrong stop solution (1N HCl instead of 2N sulfuric acid) was used in two assays (Prod-17 and Prod-18), contrary to the Analytical Procedure. Since the purpose of the stop solution is to inhibit the activity of the peroxidase enzyme with acid (by increasing the concentration of H+), no impact on the assay signals would be expected. Consistent with this expectation, Prod-17 and Prod-18 met the run acceptance criteria for the positive and negative controls comparable to other acceptable assays. Moreover, the A450nm signals from the confirmatory (unspiked) and titration (dilution at MRD) produced by the same two study samples were comparable, regardless of the stop solution used. Based on this assessment, this deviation was considered to have no impact on the validity of the study phase sample analysis results and acceptable results from Prod-17 and Prod-18 were reported.

## Sample disposition:

A total of 78 samples from 13 subjects were analysed, including 52 samples collected during Part 1 (Year 1) and 26 samples collected during Part 2 (Year 2).

From the 78 samples analysed in the screening tier, 22 samples from 5 subjects had a mean A450nm value ≥ PSCP (plate-specific cut point) and were determined to be potentially positive (The A450nm values obtained for the study samples varied from 0.065 to 0.185 for those that screened negative and from 0.140 to 0.391 for those that screened positive) and were reanalysed in the confirmatory assay.

In the confirmatory assay, 6 out of the 22 reanalysed samples were determined to be positive. All confirmed positive samples were collected from the same subject ( 001), and this subject was positive at all tested time points (Day 1 0h, Day 1 +4h, Day 150, Day 330, Day 510 and Day 720). While the total incidence of ADA was determined to be 7.7% (1 out of 13 subjects), it was noted that subject 001 displayed pre-existing reactivity since the sample collected prior to the first dose administration (Day 1 0h) was positive.

The false positive rate (FPR) at the screening level was assessed by considering 13 samples collected from 13 subjects prior to injection on Day 1 as baseline samples. Each of these baseline samples was analysed using the coating method. From 13 pre-dose baseline samples, 4 were potentially positive in the screening tier and 1 of these 4 was additionally positive in the confirmatory tier (true positive). The FPR at the screening level was determined to be 25.0% (3 confirmed negative samples/12 baseline samples with overall negative status), which was higher than the expected FPR range of 2 to 11% for study sample testing (Devanarayan et al. 2017). Given the low number of samples (13) used for the calculation, this calculated FPR may not be a reliable estimate of the FPR of the assay for the study population. Moreover, since a confirmatory assay was in place to discriminate between true and false positive, the observed FPR was considered acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The 6 samples collected from 1 subject that confirmed positive were reanalysed in the titration assay.

For all 6 study samples collected from 1 subject analysed in the titration tier, the titer values (dilution fold) ranged from &lt; 50 (MRD) to 100. Since all of the titer values from post-treatment samples were comparable (within ±1 two-fold dilution) to the pre-dose sample collected on Day 1, it was concluded that the immunogenicity response displayed by subject 001 was not treatment-related and was likely due to the presence of pre-existing cross-reactive antibodies.

Study sample analysis has been adequately reported. The confirmatory controls from reported assays met the acceptance criteria. The FPR at the screening level was calculated to be 25.0 %, substantially higher than desirable (preferably 5%). As pointed out by the Applicant, this may have been caused by the low number of samples in the calculation. Furthermore, the Applicant's reasoning that a confirmatory assay was in place to discriminate between true and false positive may be considered reassuring. During validation (Test Site Study No. 3002412), drug tolerance was established at ≤ 1 µg/mL of inclisiran at the sensitivity low positive control (sLPC) level. Therefore, analysis of any study sample with inclisiran concentrations &gt; 1 μg/mL could have yielded false-negative results. The Applicant pointed out that the drug tolerance is highly dependent upon the affinity of the positive control and may not be representative for all study samples.

## Pharmacokinetic data analysis

The evaluation of inclisiran concentrations was an exploratory endpoint for both the ORION-16 and ORION-13 studies.

Inclisiran PK parameters using noncompartmental analysis were not calculated as only sparse PK sampling was conducted at the Day 1 visit for these studies: i.e., 0 hours (pre-dose -immediately before the first administration of study drug), 4 hours post first dose, and 24 hours post first dose, respectively). Concentrations below the LLOQ were reported as zero and missing data was labelled as such in the Bioanalytical Data Report.

Inclisiran concentration-time data was summarised using descriptive statistics by sampling time point. Summary descriptive statistics included mean (arithmetic and geometric), standard deviation (SD), coefficient of variation (CV; arithmetic and geometric), median, minimum, and maximum and including the frequency (n, %) of concentrations below the LLOQ (reported as zero). The PK analysis set was used for this analysis. In ORION-16 and ORION-13, inclisiran plasma concentrations were measured only after the first dose; thus, only inter-subject, and not intra-subject, variability was assessed.

A population PK analysis was conducted to calculate inclisiran PK parameters.

## Population pharmacokinetic analysis

The popPK analysis was based on data from: four global adult studies (ALN-PCSSC-001, ORION-6, ORION-7, ORION-12), one China PKPD study (ORION-14), one Japan bridging study in ASCVD, ASCVD-risk equivalents or patients with HeFH (ORION-15), and two adolescent studies in HoFH (ORION-13) and HeFH (ORION--16). Since hepatic and renal impairment is not relevant for this analysis given the young patient populations, only subjects with normal hepatic and renal function were included from the ORION-6 and ORION-7 studies in the PK analysis. A total of 60 adolescents

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(aged 12-17 years) and 173 adults had evaluable PK and were included in the popPK analysis. Distribution of age and weight in the available dataset is depicted in Table 7.

Available samples in adolescents were sparse samples from ORION-13 and ORION-16 with three sampling time points: pre-dose, 4h and 24h post-dose. Dosing ORION-16 and ORION-13 was: Inclisiran sodium 300 mg SC administered as a single SC injection on Day 1, Day 90, Day 270. Part 2: Inclisiran sodium 300 mg SC administered as a single SC injection on Day 360 (if switching from placebo in Part 1), Day 450 and Day 630. Participants on inclisiran in part 1 receive Placebo on Day 360.

During popPK analysis, missing covariates were imputed as median. In the popPK analysis, inclisiran plasma concentration data below the LLOQ (BLQ) was handled using the M3 censoring method as described in Beal, 2001. Multivariate linear models were fit to log transformed NCA parameters, including continuous covariates as log transformed. All covariate relationships were fit simultaneously, and covariates with p-value less than 0.001 were considered for testing in the population PK analysis. Covariate effects with relative standard error (RSE) less than 30% (p-value less than 0.001) were retained in the final model. Model diagnostics of empirical bayes estimates vs covariate values were also examined to assess plausibility of additional covariate effects. A sensitivity analysis was performed by fitting the final popPK model on adult data only to determine the effects of including adolescent data into the model fitting. Population PK analyses were performed using the software.

Source: TASK7102\\_O13-16\\_popPK\\_year\\_1\\_DBL/results/001\\_weight\\_age\\_summary.csv Generated by: TASK7102\\_O13-16\\_popPK\\_year\\_1\\_DBL/scripts/001\\_explore\\_data.R

The base adult and adolescent popPK model was a one compartment model with simultaneous first and zero order absorption and first order elimination. Covariate exploration on adult NCA parameters, and adult and adolescent 4h and 24h concentration, revealed body weight as a significant covariate on almost all parameters, including 4h concentration, Cmax, AUC, CL/F and Vz/F. The final popPK model includes body weight as a covariate on clearance and volume with an estimated exponent. Model parameter estimates from base and final model are reported in Table 8, with RSE (%) given in brackets. The final popPK model was used to obtain post-hoc estimated popPK parameters, and popPK simulated individual PK profiles for the patients in ORION-13 and ORION-16. These individual PK profiles were used to calculate individual NCA PK parameters, including Cmax, Tmax, AUC0-24h, T1/2 and AUCinf.

The methods used for model development are considered standard. Covariate analyses used low pvalues (p&lt;0.001). Estimated exponents for body weight relation to clearance was 0.842 and for volume 1.73 for the combined adolescent and adult dataset. These values deviate from the empirical exponents of 0.75 for clearance and 1 for volume. But since the dataset with n=60 adolescents in the age of 12-17 years and n=173 adults is considered sufficient to estimate the exponent and model performance overall appears adequate (see below), this is considered acceptable.

## Evaluation and qualification of models

Precision of final model parameters are given in Table 8 above. VPCs from the final popPK model for dose-normalised inclisiran concentrations in adults and adolescents, stratified by body weight and age categories are given in Figure 1 and Figure 2, respectively.

/vob/CKJX839A1/mas/mas\\_7/model/pgm\\_001/TASK7102\\_013-16\\_popPK\\_year\\_1\\_DBL /scripts/KJX\\_05\\_VPCOutput.R /scripts/KJX\\_05\\_VPCOutput.Rmd /results/KJX\\_05\\_VPCOutput\\_run004.html

The Applicant did not submit the complete CSRs for the ORION-14 and ORION-15 studies but instead provided the respective bioanalytical reports. Since data of these studies were included in the PopPK analysis, it needs to be shown that the results of the analytical methods for determination of inclisiran concentrations are comparable. If different analytical methods and/or CROs have been involved, a cross-validation would be needed and should be reported. The additional explanation was provided. Method INCLHPP applied to study sample analysis in studies ORION-16 and -13 was not directly crossvalidated to original QPS method 319-1418. Instead, method comparison is to rely on indirect chain comparison only. According to weighted Deming regression, LabCorp Madison duplex assay results are generally higher than those at QPS with difference increasing with concentration. Based on the BlandAltman Plot of mean percentage difference, Labcorp Madison duplex results are lower than those for QPS in the lower concentration range (i.e. at LQC (30ng/mL) and LMQC (400ng/mL)), and higher in the higher concentration range (i.e. at MQC (5000ng/mL) and HQC (8000ng/mL)). In absence of a direct cross-validation, stronger bias than revealed for the LAbcorp duplex assay vs. the QPS duplex assay cannot be ruled out for the Labcorp Madison single strand assay. However, given that most samples reported in the ORON-16 and -13 studies had a measured concentration below 2000 ng/mL, the impact on PK data may still be acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The model purpose is to describe the concentration-time curve in adolescents and adults, to evaluate similarity of exposure between adults and adolescents and finally to define whether dosing in adolescents was adequate. For this purpose, the depicted VPCs are not considered meaningful. Observed concentrations should be depicted in addition (according to Bergstrand et al. 2011). Additionally, pcVPCs (based on non-dose-normalised concentrations) should be provided instead of VPCs with dose-normalised concentrations. Further data concerning this was provided and was considered acceptable. From the initially presented results, it could not be decided whether the proposed dose (300mg sc) is adequate for the entire group of adolescents or whether a lower body weight cut-off would be needed. Therefore, individual and predicted exposure metrics were asked to be shown with age and body weight on a continuous scale. In addition, boxplots of exposure (Cmax, Cmin, and AUC) for patients below and above 40, 45 and 50 kg were asked to be shown and the need for a lower weight cut-off discussed (depiction according to EMA paediatric Q&amp;A).

For the final popPK model, pcVPCs (based on non-dose-normalised concentrations) instead of VPCs with dose-normalised concentrations should be provided with observed concentrations depicted in addition (according to Bergstrand et al. 2011). Additionally, boxplots of exposure (for Cmax, Cmin, and AUC) for patients below and above 40, 45 and 50 kg should be shown (for depiction see EMA paediatric Q&amp;A) and the need for a lower weight cut-off discussed. The requested pcVPC was reported and is considered acceptable. The requested plots comparing exposure (Cmax and AUC) for different weight bands compared to adult exposure were also reported.

The responses provided revealed that exposures were much higher (3-fold for Cmax and AUC) in adolescents compared to adults after administration of same dose (300mg). This led to the question about adequacy of the dosing regimen for adolescent patients. In response to the 2 nd RSI, the MAH argued mainly with the benign safety profile of inclisiran, with no SAEs considered related to study drug, no new safety signals in the adolescent studies and neither in younger patients (6-12 years, with lower weights but same dose (300mg) down to 23 kg), and no sign of accumulation (also not in the liver). Therefore, the proposed dosing regimen was considered acceptable.

## 2.3.3. Pharmacokinetics

Data on dose proportionality, PK (absorption, distribution and protein binding, metabolism, excretion) of inclisiran in adults, inclisiran PK in adult special populations and in vitro enzyme/transporter DDI assessments were reviewed as part of the initial registration.

## Special populations

## Pharmacokinetics of inclisiran in adolescents (≥12 to &lt;18 years of age)

The evaluation of inclisiran concentrations in plasma was an exploratory endpoint for both the ORION16 and ORION-13 studies. For both studies, a detailed description of the study design, methods and primary, secondary and other explorative endpoints is presented in section Clinical Efficacy, subsection: Main studies.

Inclisiran PK parameters using noncompartmental analysis were not calculated as only sparse PK sampling was conducted at the Day 1 visit for these studies: i.e., 0 hours (pre-dose -immediately before the first administration of study drug), 4 hours post first dose, and 24 hours post first dose, respectively).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Inclisiran concentration-time data was summarised using descriptive statistics.

To assess whether inclisiran concentrations at these timepoints would be different from adults, concentrations at 4 and 24 hours are compared to those measured in adults at the same timepoints. Furthermore, due to the sparse PK sampling in ORION-16 and ORION-13 a population PK model was established based on adult and adolescent data to obtain individual PK parameters for adolescents in both studies. For this purpose, the final popPK model was used to obtain post-hoc estimated popPK parameters, and popPK simulated individual PK profiles for the patients in ORION-13 and ORION-16. These individual PK profiles were used to calculate individual NCA PK parameters, including Cmax, Tmax, AUC0-24h, T1/2, and AUCinf. The individual PK parameters came from [KJX839 Population PK Model].

## ORION-16 (HeFH)

After a single s.c. administration of inclisiran sodium 300 mg, variability in inclisiran plasma concentrations was low to moderate and overall higher than in the adult population. The highest inclisiran concentrations for each subject were at 4 hours after s.c. administration. At 24 hours postdose, inclisiran concentrations declined to either substantially lower levels or were below the LLOQ (&lt;10 ng/mL).

Inclisiran mean plasma concentration-time profile is shown in the figure below. Summary statistics for plasma concentration-time data following a single s.c. administration of inclisiran sodium 300 mg are presented in the table underneath.

The range of inclisiran concentrations at the 4- and 24-hour timepoints shown in the above table substantially overlap with concentrations at 4 hours (range: 371 to 1071 ng/mL, n=4) and 24 hours (12 to 206 ng/mL, n=4) reported for adults co-administered inclisiran sodium 300 mg and a statin [PK Report ALN-PCSSC-001-Table 15.1.1.1.5].

From population PK modelling of adolescent PK data that importantly provides individual PK parameters, inclisiran mean (SD) Cmax, AUC0-24hr, and AUCinf is 953 (524) ng/mL, 12300 (4640) ng*hr/mL and 12800 (4490) ng*hr/mL, respectively for ORION-16 (n=51) and 1270 (480) ng/mL, 14400 (2720) ng*hr/mL and 14700 (2670) ng*hr/mL, respectively, for ORION-13 (n=9) [KJX839 Population PK Report, Table 7 7]. For adults co-administered inclisiran sodium 300 mg and a statin, after a single inclisiran sodium 300 mg s.c. dose Cmax, AUC0-24hr, and AUCinf is 928 (375) ng/mL, 12286 (4404) ng*hr/mL and 13414 (1748) ng*hr/mL, respectively [PK Report ALN-PCSSC 001 Table 15.2.1.1.5].

## ORION-13 (HoFH)

After s.c. administration of inclisiran sodium 300 mg, variability in inclisiran plasma concentrations was low in adolescents aged 12 to &lt;18 years with HoFH and LDL-C &gt;130 mg/dL (3.4 mmol/L) on stable, individualised, optimal standard of care (SoC) background lipid-lowering therapy including maximally tolerated statin treatment, at the investigator's discretion. The highest inclisiran concentrations for each participant were at 4 hours after s.c. administration. At 24 hours post-dose, inclisiran concentrations declined to either substantially lower levels or were below the LLOQ (&lt;10 ng/mL).

Inclisiran mean plasma concentration-time profile is shown in the figure below. Summary statistics for plasma concentration-time data following a single s.c. administration of inclisiran sodium 300 mg are presented in the table underneath.

ORION-16 (HeFH): Conclusions on PK/measured plasma concentration in adolescents (12 to &lt;18 years) with a genetic and/or clinical diagnosis of HeFH and LDL-C &gt;130 mg/dL (3.4 mmol/L) on optimal SoC lipid-lowering therapy are at present considered preliminary. While it is agreed that plasma concentrations as reported in the ORION-16 study in n=40 adolescents (12 to &lt;18 years) with a genetic and/or clinical diagnosis of HeFH and LDL-C &gt;130 mg/dL (3.4 mmol/L) on optimal SoC lipidlowering therapy overlap with the plasma concentrations reported in n=4 adults co-administered inclisiran sodium 300 mg and a statin in early PK study ALN-PCSSC-001, it should be noted that besides the very low number of only n=4 adults on whom the comparison is to rely, the highest concentration determined in the paediatric population at 4 hours postdose is 3010ng/mL. Furthermore, based on population PK modelling, exposures are overall higher in the paediatric population than in the adult population. Of note, different methods and different contract facilities were involved in the quantification of inclisiran in plasma in the two studies and methods were not directly cross-validated. Indirect method comparison has to rely on an additionally established assay not yet used for study sample analysis having to serve as \"bridge\". Proportional and fixed bias was revealed in thes indirect cross validations. Since most concentrations in the ORION-16 study were below 2000 ng/mL, the impact on PK may be considered overall acceptable (see section on bioanalytics).

Of note, regarding the ORION-16 protocol Amendment 2, dated 17-Feb-2023, it was stated that the purpose of the amendment was to add an interim analysis (IA) for the PK data collected on Day 1, to update a PK extrapolation to paediatrics, i.e., to further refine the extrapolation from adult PK data with adolescent PK data, covering a lower range of body weights and ages. The updated PK extrapolation was seen critical as part of developing a dose selection strategy for planned future paediatric studies in younger children (&lt;12 years). The PK IA was performed by a separate team (not involved in any other study activities) before the Part 1/Year 1 database lock, when the study was unblinded as per protocol. No further information on the results of the IA, incl. the updated PK extrapolation could be located.

ORION-13 (HoFH): The Applicant's conclusion that plasma concentrations as reported in the ORION-13 study in n=9 adolescents (12 to &lt;18 years) with a diagnosis of HoFH and LDL-C &gt; 130 mg/dL (3.4 mmol/L) on optimal SoC lipid-lowering therapy substantially overlap with the plasma concentrations reported in n=4 adults co-administered inclisiran sodium 300 mg and a statin in early PK study ALN- PCSSC-001 could not be fully followed. While minimum plasma concentration reported in the adolescent ORION-13 study population is within the range of concentrations reported in the adult ALNPCSSC-001 study population, the maximum concentration reported is almost doubled, and higher values are reported also for mean and median. Uncertainty regarding the informative value of the comparison pertains to the low number of patients for whom plasma concentration information is available, i.e. only n=9 adolescents from study ORION-13 and n=4 adults from study ALN-PCSSC-001. As noted for the ORION-16 study above, different methods and different contract facilities were involved in the quantification of inclisiran in plasma in the two studies compared. As noted above, only indirect cross-validation is available. Similarly to the ORION-16 study, regarding the ORION-13 protocol Amendment 2, dated 17-Feb-2023, it was stated that the purpose of the amendment was to add an interim analysis (IA) for the PK data collected on Day 1, to update a PK extrapolation to paediatrics, i.e., to further refine the extrapolation from adult PK data with adolescent PK data, covering a lower range of body weights and ages. The updated PK extrapolation was seen critical as part of developing a dose selection strategy for planned future paediatric studies in younger children (&lt;12 years). The PK IA was performed by a separate team (not involved in any other study activities) before the Part 1/Year 1 database lock, when the study was unblinded as per protocol.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.3.4. Discussion on clinical pharmacology

In ORION-16 and in ORION-13 Studies, PK samples were collected pre-dose (immediately before the first administration of study drug), 4- and 24-hours after the first dose. Inclisiran was quantified using a validated liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) method with the Lower Limit of Quantification (LLOQ) of 10 ng/mL. In both studies, the highest inclisiran concentrations were observed at 4 hours after s.c. administration. At 24 hours post-dose, inclisiran concentrations declined to either lower levels or below the LLOQ.

Validation Report Quantitative determination of A-120190 and A-122088 in human plasma by LCMS/MS from 25-Aug-2022 and Method validation report Amendment No. 1 from 19-Oct-2022 have been submitted, as were the respective bioanalytical reports. The acceptance criteria provided are in line with the Guideline on bioanalytical method validation [EMEA/CHMP/EWP/192217/2009 Rev. 1 Corr. 2**]. The method can be considered adequate for determination of A-120190 and A-122088 in human plasma and with acceptable performance in both studies.

To support conclusions on inclisiran PK/plasma concentration data reported in the ORION-16 and ORION-13 studies as regards their comparability with earlier PK data reported in adults in the PK studies supporting the initial marketing authorisation of Leqvio, as well as with recent inclisiran PK data in Japanese and Chinese subjects, and as regards extrapolation efforts, these are hampered by the fact that the bioanalytical sites and the bioanalytical methods used for quantification of inclisiran in the paediatric vs. adult populations differ and methods were not directly cross-validated. Instead, method comparisons are to rely on chains of separate cross-validations involving further assays that were not yet used for study sample analysis to serve as \"bridges\". Proportional and fixed bias were revealed in statistical analyses for some of the cross-validations. Overall, results from the LabCorp Madison site are generally higher than those at the original QPS site with difference increasing with concentration. Based on Bland-Altman Plot of mean percentage difference, while the overall mean is higher, Labcorp Madison results are lower than those for QPS in the lower concentration range (i.e. at LQC (30ng/mL) and LMQC (400ng/mL)) and higher in the higher concentration range (i.e. at MQC (5000ng/mL) and HQC (8000ng/mL)).

In absence of direct cross-validations, stronger bias than revealed in the indirect comparisons cannot be ruled out. However, given that most samples reported in the ORON-16 and -13 studies had a measured concentration below 2000 ng/mL, the impact on PK data was considered acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A population PK analysis was performed to describe inclisiran exposure over time in adults and adolescents and to calculate individual PK parameters for adolescents in ORION-13 and ORION-16. Based on the analysis that showed that although PK was significantly correlated with body weight, the body weight distribution and the concentrations in adolescents and adults overlapped, no dose adjustment was considered necessary for adolescents.

## 2.3.5. Conclusions on clinical pharmacology

Overall, with the limitations noted, the provided clinical pharmacology data package can be considered acceptable. Slight amendment of the SmPC to reflect that inclisiran plasma concentrations were higher in paediatric patients than those measured in adults has been agreed with the Applicant.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.4. Clinical efficacy

## 2.4.1. Dose response study(ies)

N/A

## 2.4.2. Main studies

ORION-13 (CKJX839C12302; EudraCT No.: 2020-002755-38): \"A Two part (double-blind inclisiran versus placebo [Year 1] followed by open-label inclisiran [Year 2]) randomised multicenter study to evaluate safety, tolerability, and efficacy of inclisiran in adolescents (12 to less than 18 years) with homozygous familial hypercholesterolemia (HoFH) and elevated LDL-cholesterol\"

Study ORION-13 evaluated the safety, tolerability, and efficacy of inclisiran in adolescents from 12 to less than 18 years of age with homozygous familial hypercholesterolemia (HoFH) and elevated lowdensity lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C). It is part of the inclisiran paediatric program as agreed with the European Medicines Agency (EMA) in the paediatric investigation plan (PIP; EMEA-002214-PIP01-17M02, P/0322/2023) and intended to support the registration of inclisiran in adolescents with HoFH.

The study was conducted in two-parts, a double-blind inclisiran versus placebo part (Year 1), followed by an open-label inclisiran part (Year 2), as a randomised, multicentre study in adolescents with HoFH and LDL-C &gt;130 mg/dL (3.4 mmol/L) on stable, individualised, optimal standard of care (SoC) background lipid-lowering therapy including maximally tolerated statin treatment, at the investigator's discretion.

To explore the pharmacokinetics (PK) of inclisiran in adolescents with HoFH, the study also included PK and pharmacogenetic assessments. PK assessment was based on sparse PK sampling (i.e. 3 samples at the Day 1 visit; pre-dose immediately before the first administration of study drug, 4-, and 24-hours after first dose; see section Pharmacokinetics). Optional pharmacogenetic analyses included analyses for potential mutations in the LDLR, ApoB, PCSK9, and LDLR adaptor protein 1 (LDLRAP1) genes.

The study design is summarised in the following table.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Table 11: Summary of study design

Two-part, randomised, multicentre, international Phase III study. The study had two sequential parts:

- Part 1/Year 1: 12 months double-blind, parallel group period, in which participants were randomised in a 2:1 ratio to receive either inclisiran sodium 300 mg s.c. or placebo. The primary endpoint was assessed at Day 330.
- Part 2 / Year 2: 12 months single arm, open-label follow-up period, with all participants receiving inclisiran sodium 300 mg s.c.
* Primary endpoint assessed at Day 330.
- # \"Switch Day\": Participants randomised to placebo in Part 1 started to receive inclisiran on Day 360. Participants randomised to inclisiran in Part 1 received placebo on Day 360 only to maintain the blind for Part 1. [Study ORION-13-Figure 9-1]

ORION-13 Study Design

<!-- image -->

## Methods

## Study participants

The study population consisted of adolescents (male or female; ≥12 to &lt;18 years at screening) with a genetically confirmed diagnosis of HoFH (excluding LDLR null/null genotypes) and on a maximally tolerated dose of statin (at investigator's discretion) with or without other lipid lowering therapy (e.g., ezetimibe).

## Key inclusion criteria were:

- HoFH diagnosed by genetic confirmation excluding LDLR null/null genotypes,
- fasting LDL-C &gt;130 mg/dL (3.4 mmol/L) at screening,
- on maximally tolerated dose of statin (Investigator's discretion) with or without other lipidlowering therapy (e.g., ezetimibe),
- participants on lipid-lowering therapies (such as statin and/or e.g., ezetimibe) should have been on stable dose ≥30 days before screening with no planned medication or dose changes during study participation,
- participants on documented regimen of LDL-apheresis were allowed to continue apheresis during study if needed (schedule not allowed to change during double-blind period),
- estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) &gt;30 mL/min/1.73 m 2 at screening.

## Key exclusion criteria were:

- documented evidence of a null (negative) mutation in both LDLR alleles,
- previous treatment (≤90 days of screening) with monoclonal antibodies towards PCSK9,
- history of poor response to therapy with any monoclonal antibody directed towards PCSK9,
- treatment with mipomersen or lomitapide (≤5 months of screening),

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- active liver disease or unexplained, confirmed ALT or AST elevation &gt;3 × ULN, or total bilirubin elevation &gt;2 × ULN (except Gilbert's syndrome) at screening,
- pregnant or nursing females,
- HeFH
- recent or planned use of other investigational medicinal products or devices,
- secondary hypercholesterolemia (e.g., hypothyroidism or nephrotic syndrome),
- major cardiovascular AEs ≤1 month prior to randomisation.

The study included male and female adolescents from ≥12 to &lt;18 years of age with a genetic confirmation of HoFH, excluding LDLR null/null genotypes, and an LDL-C &gt;130 mg/dL who were on a maximally tolerated dose of an HMG-CoA reductase inhibitor (statin) with or without other lipid lowering therapy (excluding PCSK9 inhibitors, mipomersen, and lomitapide).

Patients with heterozygous familial hypercholesterolemia (HeFH) or secondary hypercholesterolemia were excluded.

## Treatments

In part 1 (year 1), participants received either commercially available inclisiran sodium 300 mg (equivalent to 284 mg inclisiran) in 1.5 mL solution (batch numbers 2040834, 2040329, 2041729) or a placebo formulation to the active drug formulation (batch numbers 2041390, 2040833, 2041156); during part 2 (year 2), all participants received inclisiran (batch numbers 2040329, 2040921, 2041729, 2047184).

Placebo was supplied as sterile normal saline (0.9% sodium chloride in water for injection) and administered as a 1.5 mL s.c. injection to match the dose in the inclisiran arm; the placebo pre-filled syringes were blinded and were identical in appearance to their active counterparts.

During Part 1, participants received either inclisiran at the licensed standard dose and posology for adults or placebo. During Part 2, all participants received standard dose inclisiran.

## Objectives, outcomes, endpoints

During the double-blind Part 1 (year 1) of the study, the primary efficacy and safety data comparing inclisiran to placebo were collected. The open-label single arm Part 2 (year 2) of the study collected longer-term data on inclisiran with a focus on longer-term safety.

Objectives and endpoints of study ORION-16 are summarised in the table below.
